People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure I’d call it enjoyment. At the start maybe there’s this… like, pull toward it, like it feels hard to stop thinking about the food. But during it, I don’t know if I’m enjoying it exactly or just doing it.

Sometimes I barely even notice what it tastes like, which sounds weird because with animals I can be so tuned in—like if I’m prepping diets and one of the reptiles needs something specific, I’m careful about every detail. But with me it’s kind of the opposite. I’m just eating and then suddenly realizing how much is gone.

So… I don’t know. Maybe a little at first, but mostly it feels automatic, not like sitting down and enjoying a meal.

I don’t know if “pleased” is the word I’d use. I mean… not really? It’s hard to say because afterward I’m usually just sort of aware that it happened, and then my mind jumps to something else.

Sometimes I just kind of sit there and feel stupid, like, “Why did I do that?” But then I also start thinking about needing to clean up, or if I left the kitchen light on, or whether I remembered to refill the water for the rabbits at work—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how to describe it. It doesn’t feel good afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most people would eat in a similar time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Especially after late shifts. I’ll get home and it’s like I haven’t eaten in days, even if I have. I’ve had nights where I eat a whole frozen pizza, then cereal straight from the box, then whatever leftovers are in the fridge, and then I’m digging through the cabinet for snacks.\n\nIt’s usually all within, I don’t know, an hour or two. And I know it’s more than I’d ever eat if someone else were there. Like, I’d be mortified eating that much in front of anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any physical symptoms, like stomach discomfort, after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I feel kind of gross afterward, but I don’t know if I pay attention to it as, like, a physical symptom. I usually get distracted by cleaning up or trying to make the kitchen look normal again.\n\nStomach discomfort… maybe? I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing. I guess I haven’t really tracked it. At work I notice every tiny change in the animals, like if a ferret isn’t eating or a bird’s posture is off, but with myself I kind of just… ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. What do you mean by issues, exactly? I’m not really… I don’t know, I don’t think I’ve noticed anything specific there.\n\nI guess it’s not something I pay close attention to. I haven’t noticed a specific problem, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a lack of interest in sex or difficulty becoming sexually aroused?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I haven’t noticed a specific lack of interest, I guess, or difficulty like that, but it’s also not something I’m checking in on very much.\n\nMost days I’m just kind of on autopilot—work, feeding schedules, cleaning enclosures, making sure I didn’t forget some tiny thing. At the start maybe there’s this… like, pull toward it, like it feels hard to stop thinking about the food. But during it, I don’t know if I’m enjoying it exactly or just doing it.\n\nSometimes I barely even notice what it tastes like, which sounds weird because with animals I can be so tuned in—like if I’m prepping diets and one of the reptiles needs something specific, I’m careful about every detail. But with me it’s kind of the opposite. I’m just eating and then suddenly realizing how much is gone.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little at first, but mostly it feels automatic, not like sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleased or satisfied with yourself after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “pleased” is the word I’d use. I mean… not really? With this, it’s not like that. It’s not satisfying in that clean, finished way. Yeah… I think it has, at least at work. I’m not really sure it’s pleasure, exactly. I mean, sometimes the food tastes good at first, I guess, but during those episodes I’m not sitting there like… savoring it, the way you would with a nice meal.

It’s more like I notice the food in a quick way, and then my mind kind of goes somewhere else. Like at work when I’m feeding the reptiles and I’m thinking through the whole checklist instead of what I’m actually doing—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison.

So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it in a normal way. The taste is there, but I’m not sure I’m getting real pleasure from it.

I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “Oh, I’m definitely less interested,” but I also don’t keep track of that very well.

Guilty? I… I don’t know if that’s the word I’d use every time.

Sometimes afterward I’m just kind of sitting there thinking, “What was that?” Not in a big dramatic way necessarily. It’s more like when I realize I forgot to latch something properly at work and then I keep replaying it in my head, even if nothing bad happened. Except with food it’s harder to know what I’m replaying, if that makes sense.

So maybe there’s some guilt mixed in, but I’m not totally sure. I notice feeling off afterward, but I don’t always label it as guilty.

Like last week I kept thinking about one of the older cats who wasn’t acting right, and that stayed in my head all night. So when you ask about sex, I’m sort of like… I don’t know, it hasn’t been at the front of my mind.\n\nSo, no clear problem that I can point to, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the food or the act of eating during these episodes when you feel like you're losing control?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it enjoyment. At the start maybe there’s this… like, pull toward it, like it feels hard to stop thinking about the food. But during it, I don’t know if I’m enjoying it exactly or just doing it.\n\nSometimes I barely even notice what it tastes like, which sounds weird because with animals I can be so tuned in—like if I’m prepping diets and one of the reptiles needs something specific, I’m careful about every detail. But with me it’s kind of the opposite. I’m just eating and then suddenly realizing how much is gone.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little at first, but mostly it feels automatic, not like sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleased or satisfied with yourself after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “pleased” is the word I’d use. I mean… not really? It’s hard to say because afterward I’m usually just sort of aware that it happened, and then my mind jumps to something else.\n\nLike at work, if I finish cleaning the kennels there’s this clear feeling of, okay, that’s done, everything’s in order. With this, it’s not like that. It’s not satisfying in that clean, finished way. But I’m also not sure I can describe exactly what I feel afterward.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I feel pleased with myself. I just don’t have a neat answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, at least at work. There was this bilingual outreach presentation we had to do—well, I was helping with it, and I usually can manage that kind of thing okay, even if I get nervous. But I was so distracted thinking about leaving afterward that I kind of stumbled through parts of it.\n\nAnd then of course I started worrying that my coworkers noticed, which made it worse. I don’t know if they actually did. They might’ve just been focused on the handouts or the animals we were talking about—there was this whole section about proper habitat setup, and people always ask a lot of questions there—but in my head I was like, they can tell I’m not fully here.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it’s affected work some. Relationships, I’m less sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your sexual desire?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking that, honestly.\n\nI guess with animals it’s easy to notice changes because there’s a routine—like if one of the birds stops eating or isn’t acting normal, you can compare it to yesterday. With me, things are less… measurable, I guess.\n\nSo, decreased sexual desire? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything obvious, but I also don’t think I’ve paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disgusted or repelled by sexual activity?\n\nPatient: Repelled? I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say that. I guess I haven’t really thought about it in those words.\n\nLike, “disgusted” makes me think of cleaning cages when something’s gone really wrong, you know, like there’s a smell and your whole body reacts before you even think about it. I don’t think sexual activity feels like that to me. But I also don’t know if I’ve been paying enough attention to answer clearly.\n\nSo… no, not disgusted exactly. I’m just not totally sure what I do feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you enjoy the taste of the food or get pleasure from eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s pleasure, exactly. I mean, sometimes the food tastes good at first, I guess, but during those episodes I’m not sitting there like… savoring it, the way you would with a nice meal.\n\nIt’s more like I notice the food in a quick way, and then my mind kind of goes somewhere else. Like at work when I’m feeding the reptiles and I’m thinking through the whole checklist instead of what I’m actually doing—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it in a normal way. The taste is there, but I’m not sure I’m getting real pleasure from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself having less interest in sexual activity lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “Oh, I’m definitely less interested,” but I also don’t keep track of that very well.\n\nLately my head’s been kind of crowded, and when I’m at work there’s always something to focus on—feeding schedules, cleaning, making sure the animals are acting normal. I haven’t noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always notice it as “distress” right away, but it does get into other parts of my day.

Like at work recently, we were doing this bilingual outreach presentation—well, not “we” exactly, I had a part in it—and I kept thinking about leaving afterward, like just getting out of there, and my mind wouldn’t stay on what I was saying. I stumbled through a few parts, which is embarrassing because usually I can talk about the animals pretty comfortably. Then afterward I kept wondering if my coworkers noticed.

So… yeah, I guess it is causing distress. It’s not just staying contained to eating.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main thing that made me come in.

It’s usually when I’m by myself at night, and once I start eating it feels like… I don’t know, like something else has the steering wheel. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, put it away, stop now,” and then I’m still standing there eating anyway.

It’s weird because at work I’m pretty good at stopping and starting tasks—like if one enclosure needs cleaning and then something else comes up, I can switch gears. But with this, once it’s started, it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I guess I came in because eating has been getting kind of out of hand. Mostly at night, when I’m by myself. I’ll start eating and it’s like something just takes over, like I’m watching myself do it but I can’t really stop, even while I’m thinking, “Put it away, just put the food away.”\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it like that. I work with animals, so I’m used to routines and feeding schedules and all that, and I can follow those just fine, but with myself at home it’s different. I don’t know, it’s embarrassing. I just feel like I lose control once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I don’t know if guilt is the exact word. Sometimes I just kind of sit there and feel stupid, like, “Why did I do that?” But then I also start thinking about needing to clean up, or if I left the kitchen light on, or whether I remembered to refill the water for the rabbits at work—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how to describe it. It doesn’t feel good afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most people would eat in a similar time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Especially after late shifts. I’ll get home and it’s like I haven’t eaten in days, even if I have. I’ve had nights where I eat a whole frozen pizza, then cereal straight from the box, then whatever leftovers are in the fridge, and then I’m digging through the cabinet for snacks.\n\nIt’s usually all within, I don’t know, an hour or two. And I know it’s more than I’d ever eat if someone else were there. Like, I’d be mortified eating that much in front of anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any physical symptoms, like stomach discomfort, after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I feel kind of gross afterward, but I don’t know if I pay attention to it as, like, a physical symptom. I usually get distracted by cleaning up or trying to make the kitchen look normal again.\n\nStomach discomfort… maybe? I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing. I guess I haven’t really tracked it. At work I notice every tiny change in the animals, like if a ferret isn’t eating or a bird’s posture is off, but with myself I kind of just… ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. What do you mean by issues, exactly? I’m not really… I don’t know, I don’t think I’ve noticed anything specific there.\n\nI guess it’s not something I pay close attention to. My schedule gets weird with the animal care shifts, and sometimes I’m just tired or distracted, like I’ll be thinking about whether I locked the feed room or if one of the rabbits was acting off that day. Sorry—that’s probably not what you meant.\n\nSo… I don’t really have a clear answer. I haven’t noticed a specific problem, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a lack of interest in sex or difficulty becoming sexually aroused?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I haven’t noticed a specific lack of interest, I guess, or difficulty like that, but it’s also not something I’m checking in on very much.\n\nMost days I’m just kind of on autopilot—work, feeding schedules, cleaning enclosures, making sure I didn’t forget some tiny thing. Like last week I kept thinking about one of the older cats who wasn’t acting right, and that stayed in my head all night. So when you ask about sex, I’m sort of like… I don’t know, it hasn’t been at the front of my mind.\n\nSo, no clear problem that I can point to, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the food or the act of eating during these episodes when you feel like you're losing control?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it enjoyment. At the start maybe there’s this… like, pull toward it, like it feels hard to stop thinking about the food. But during it, I don’t know if I’m enjoying it exactly or just doing it.\n\nSometimes I barely even notice what it tastes like, which sounds weird because with animals I can be so tuned in—like if I’m prepping diets and one of the reptiles needs something specific, I’m careful about every detail. But with me it’s kind of the opposite. I’m just eating and then suddenly realizing how much is gone.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little at first, but mostly it feels automatic, not like sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleased or satisfied with yourself after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “pleased” is the word I’d use. I mean… not really? It’s hard to say because afterward I’m usually just sort of aware that it happened, and then my mind jumps to something else.\n\nLike at work, if I finish cleaning the kennels there’s this clear feeling of, okay, that’s done, everything’s in order. With this, it’s not like that. It’s not satisfying in that clean, finished way. But I’m also not sure I can describe exactly what I feel afterward.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I feel pleased with myself. I just don’t have a neat answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, at least at work. There was this bilingual outreach presentation we had to do—well, I was helping with it, and I usually can manage that kind of thing okay, even if I get nervous. But I was so distracted thinking about leaving afterward that I kind of stumbled through parts of it.\n\nAnd then of course I started worrying that my coworkers noticed, which made it worse. I don’t know if they actually did. They might’ve just been focused on the handouts or the animals we were talking about—there was this whole section about proper habitat setup, and people always ask a lot of questions there—but in my head I was like, they can tell I’m not fully here.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it’s affected work some. Relationships, I’m less sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your sexual desire?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking that, honestly.\n\nI guess with animals it’s easy to notice changes because there’s a routine—like if one of the birds stops eating or isn’t acting normal, you can compare it to yesterday. With me, things are less… measurable, I guess.\n\nSo, decreased sexual desire? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything obvious, but I also don’t think I’ve paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disgusted or repelled by sexual activity?\n\nPatient: Repelled? I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say that. I guess I haven’t really thought about it in those words.\n\nLike, “disgusted” makes me think of cleaning cages when something’s gone really wrong, you know, like there’s a smell and your whole body reacts before you even think about it. I don’t think sexual activity feels like that to me. But I also don’t know if I’ve been paying enough attention to answer clearly.\n\nSo… no, not disgusted exactly. I’m just not totally sure what I do feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you enjoy the taste of the food or get pleasure from eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s pleasure, exactly. I mean, sometimes the food tastes good at first, I guess, but during those episodes I’m not sitting there like… savoring it, the way you would with a nice meal.\n\nIt’s more like I notice the food in a quick way, and then my mind kind of goes somewhere else. Like at work when I’m feeding the reptiles and I’m thinking through the whole checklist instead of what I’m actually doing—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t sit there timing myself or anything.

Sometimes it feels like I sort of look up and realize the food is gone, but I don’t know if that means I was eating really fast or just not paying attention. I work with animals, so I’m used to routines and feeding schedules and all that, and I can follow those just fine, but with myself at home it’s different. I don’t know, it’s embarrassing. I just feel like I lose control once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I don’t know if guilt is the exact word. Sometimes I just kind of sit there and feel stupid, like, “Why did I do that?” But then I also start thinking about needing to clean up, or if I left the kitchen light on, or whether I remembered to refill the water for the rabbits at work—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how to describe it. It doesn’t feel good afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most people would eat in a similar time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Especially after late shifts. I’ll get home and it’s like I haven’t eaten in days, even if I have. I’ve had nights where I eat a whole frozen pizza, then cereal straight from the box, then whatever leftovers are in the fridge, and then I’m digging through the cabinet for snacks.\n\nIt’s usually all within, I don’t know, an hour or two. And I know it’s more than I’d ever eat if someone else were there. Like, I’d be mortified eating that much in front of anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any physical symptoms, like stomach discomfort, after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I feel kind of gross afterward, but I don’t know if I pay attention to it as, like, a physical symptom. I usually get distracted by cleaning up or trying to make the kitchen look normal again.\n\nStomach discomfort… maybe? I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing. I guess I haven’t really tracked it. At work I notice every tiny change in the animals, like if a ferret isn’t eating or a bird’s posture is off, but with myself I kind of just… ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Like last week I kept thinking about one of the older cats who wasn’t acting right, and that stayed in my head all night. So when you ask about sex, I’m sort of like… I don’t know, it hasn’t been at the front of my mind.\n\nSo, no clear problem that I can point to, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the food or the act of eating during these episodes when you feel like you're losing control?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it enjoyment. At the start maybe there’s this… like, pull toward it, like it feels hard to stop thinking about the food. But during it, I don’t know if I’m enjoying it exactly or just doing it.\n\nSometimes I barely even notice what it tastes like, which sounds weird because with animals I can be so tuned in—like if I’m prepping diets and one of the reptiles needs something specific, I’m careful about every detail. But with me it’s kind of the opposite. I’m just eating and then suddenly realizing how much is gone.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little at first, but mostly it feels automatic, not like sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleased or satisfied with yourself after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “pleased” is the word I’d use. I mean… not really? It’s hard to say because afterward I’m usually just sort of aware that it happened, and then my mind jumps to something else.\n\nLike at work, if I finish cleaning the kennels there’s this clear feeling of, okay, that’s done, everything’s in order. With this, it’s not like that. It’s not satisfying in that clean, finished way. But I’m also not sure I can describe exactly what I feel afterward.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I feel pleased with myself. I just don’t have a neat answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, at least at work. There was this bilingual outreach presentation we had to do—well, I was helping with it, and I usually can manage that kind of thing okay, even if I get nervous. But I was so distracted thinking about leaving afterward that I kind of stumbled through parts of it.\n\nAnd then of course I started worrying that my coworkers noticed, which made it worse. I don’t know if they actually did. They might’ve just been focused on the handouts or the animals we were talking about—there was this whole section about proper habitat setup, and people always ask a lot of questions there—but in my head I was like, they can tell I’m not fully here.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it’s affected work some. Relationships, I’m less sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your sexual desire?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking that, honestly.\n\nI guess with animals it’s easy to notice changes because there’s a routine—like if one of the birds stops eating or isn’t acting normal, you can compare it to yesterday. With me, things are less… measurable, I guess.\n\nSo, decreased sexual desire? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything obvious, but I also don’t think I’ve paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disgusted or repelled by sexual activity?\n\nPatient: Repelled? I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say that. I guess I haven’t really thought about it in those words.\n\nLike, “disgusted” makes me think of cleaning cages when something’s gone really wrong, you know, like there’s a smell and your whole body reacts before you even think about it. I don’t think sexual activity feels like that to me. But I also don’t know if I’ve been paying enough attention to answer clearly.\n\nSo… no, not disgusted exactly. I’m just not totally sure what I do feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you enjoy the taste of the food or get pleasure from eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s pleasure, exactly. I mean, sometimes the food tastes good at first, I guess, but during those episodes I’m not sitting there like… savoring it, the way you would with a nice meal.\n\nIt’s more like I notice the food in a quick way, and then my mind kind of goes somewhere else. Like at work when I’m feeding the reptiles and I’m thinking through the whole checklist instead of what I’m actually doing—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it in a normal way. The taste is there, but I’m not sure I’m getting real pleasure from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself having less interest in sexual activity lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “Oh, I’m definitely less interested,” but I also don’t keep track of that very well.\n\nLately my head’s been kind of crowded, and when I’m at work there’s always something to focus on—feeding schedules, cleaning, making sure the animals are acting normal. It’s easier to notice if a snake refuses food than to notice what’s going on with me, I guess.\n\nSo… less interest in sexual activity? I’m not sure. Maybe, maybe not. But with me it’s kind of the opposite. I’m just eating and then suddenly realizing how much is gone.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little at first, but mostly it feels automatic, not like sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleased or satisfied with yourself after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “pleased” is the word I’d use. I mean… not really? It’s hard to say because afterward I’m usually just sort of aware that it happened, and then my mind jumps to something else.\n\nLike at work, if I finish cleaning the kennels there’s this clear feeling of, okay, that’s done, everything’s in order. With this, it’s not like that. It’s not satisfying in that clean, finished way. But I’m also not sure I can describe exactly what I feel afterward.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I feel pleased with myself. I just don’t have a neat answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, at least at work. There was this bilingual outreach presentation we had to do—well, I was helping with it, and I usually can manage that kind of thing okay, even if I get nervous. But I was so distracted thinking about leaving afterward that I kind of stumbled through parts of it.\n\nAnd then of course I started worrying that my coworkers noticed, which made it worse. I don’t know if they actually did. They might’ve just been focused on the handouts or the animals we were talking about—there was this whole section about proper habitat setup, and people always ask a lot of questions there—but in my head I was like, they can tell I’m not fully here.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it’s affected work some. Relationships, I’m less sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your sexual desire?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking that, honestly.\n\nI guess with animals it’s easy to notice changes because there’s a routine—like if one of the birds stops eating or isn’t acting normal, you can compare it to yesterday. With me, things are less… measurable, I guess.\n\nSo, decreased sexual desire? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything obvious, but I also don’t think I’ve paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disgusted or repelled by sexual activity?\n\nPatient: Repelled? I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say that. I guess I haven’t really thought about it in those words.\n\nLike, “disgusted” makes me think of cleaning cages when something’s gone really wrong, you know, like there’s a smell and your whole body reacts before you even think about it. I don’t think sexual activity feels like that to me. But I also don’t know if I’ve been paying enough attention to answer clearly.\n\nSo… no, not disgusted exactly. I’m just not totally sure what I do feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you enjoy the taste of the food or get pleasure from eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s pleasure, exactly. I mean, sometimes the food tastes good at first, I guess, but during those episodes I’m not sitting there like… savoring it, the way you would with a nice meal.\n\nIt’s more like I notice the food in a quick way, and then my mind kind of goes somewhere else. Like at work when I’m feeding the reptiles and I’m thinking through the whole checklist instead of what I’m actually doing—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it in a normal way. The taste is there, but I’m not sure I’m getting real pleasure from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself having less interest in sexual activity lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “Oh, I’m definitely less interested,” but I also don’t keep track of that very well.\n\nLately my head’s been kind of crowded, and when I’m at work there’s always something to focus on—feeding schedules, cleaning, making sure the animals are acting normal. It’s easier to notice if a snake refuses food than to notice what’s going on with me, I guess.\n\nSo… less interest in sexual activity? I’m not sure. Maybe, maybe not. I haven’t noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I… I don’t know if that’s the word I’d use every time.\n\nSometimes afterward I’m just kind of sitting there thinking, “What was that?” Not in a big dramatic way necessarily. It’s more like when I realize I forgot to latch something properly at work and then I keep replaying it in my head, even if nothing bad happened. Except with food it’s harder to know what I’m replaying, if that makes sense.\n\nSo maybe there’s some guilt mixed in, but I’m not totally sure. I notice feeling off afterward, but I don’t always label it as guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your eating habits are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always notice it as “distress” right away, but it does get into other parts of my day.\n\nLike at work recently, we were doing this bilingual outreach presentation—well, not “we” exactly, I had a part in it—and I kept thinking about leaving afterward, like just getting out of there, and my mind wouldn’t stay on what I was saying. I stumbled through a few parts, which is embarrassing because usually I can talk about the animals pretty comfortably. Then afterward I kept wondering if my coworkers noticed.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it is causing distress. It’s not just staying contained to eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main thing that made me come in.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m by myself at night, and once I start eating it feels like… I don’t know, like something else has the steering wheel. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, put it away, stop now,” and then I’m still standing there eating anyway.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I’m pretty good at stopping and starting tasks—like if one enclosure needs cleaning and then something else comes up, I can switch gears. But with this, once it’s started, it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t sit there timing myself or anything.\n\nSometimes it feels like I sort of look up and realize the food is gone, but I don’t know if that means I was eating really fast or just not paying attention. It’s kind of like when I’m feeding the animals and I’ve done the same steps so many times that suddenly I’m at the end of the row and I don’t remember every single bucket—except this is… different, obviously.\n\nSo, yeah, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed the speed as much as the fact that it happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m… not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean, afterward I don’t feel great about it, but I get kind of stuck on the practical stuff first, like cleaning up or making sure there isn’t food out where bugs could get into it.

I work with animals, so I’m used to routines and feeding schedules and all that, and I can follow those just fine, but with myself at home it’s different. I don’t know, it’s embarrassing. I just feel like I lose control once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I don’t know if guilt is the exact word. Sometimes I just kind of sit there and feel stupid, like, “Why did I do that?” But then I also start thinking about needing to clean up, or if I left the kitchen light on, or whether I remembered to refill the water for the rabbits at work—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how to describe it. It doesn’t feel good afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most people would eat in a similar time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Especially after late shifts. I’ll get home and it’s like I haven’t eaten in days, even if I have. I’ve had nights where I eat a whole frozen pizza, then cereal straight from the box, then whatever leftovers are in the fridge, and then I’m digging through the cabinet for snacks.\n\nIt’s usually all within, I don’t know, an hour or two. And I know it’s more than I’d ever eat if someone else were there. Like, I’d be mortified eating that much in front of anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any physical symptoms, like stomach discomfort, after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I feel kind of gross afterward, but I don’t know if I pay attention to it as, like, a physical symptom. I usually get distracted by cleaning up or trying to make the kitchen look normal again.\n\nStomach discomfort… maybe? I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing. I guess I haven’t really tracked it. At work I notice every tiny change in the animals, like if a ferret isn’t eating or a bird’s posture is off, but with myself I kind of just… ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. What do you mean by issues, exactly? I’m not really… I don’t know, I don’t think I’ve noticed anything specific there.\n\nI guess it’s not something I pay close attention to. My schedule gets weird with the animal care shifts, and sometimes I’m just tired or distracted, like I’ll be thinking about whether I locked the feed room or if one of the rabbits was acting off that day. Sorry—that’s probably not what you meant.\n\nSo… I don’t really have a clear answer. I haven’t noticed a specific problem, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a lack of interest in sex or difficulty becoming sexually aroused?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I haven’t noticed a specific lack of interest, I guess, or difficulty like that, but it’s also not something I’m checking in on very much.\n\nMost days I’m just kind of on autopilot—work, feeding schedules, cleaning enclosures, making sure I didn’t forget some tiny thing. Like last week I kept thinking about one of the older cats who wasn’t acting right, and that stayed in my head all night. So when you ask about sex, I’m sort of like… I don’t know, it hasn’t been at the front of my mind.\n\nSo, no clear problem that I can point to, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the food or the act of eating during these episodes when you feel like you're losing control?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it enjoyment. At the start maybe there’s this… like, pull toward it, like it feels hard to stop thinking about the food. But during it, I don’t know if I’m enjoying it exactly or just doing it.\n\nSometimes I barely even notice what it tastes like, which sounds weird because with animals I can be so tuned in—like if I’m prepping diets and one of the reptiles needs something specific, I’m careful about every detail. But with me it’s kind of the opposite. I’m just eating and then suddenly realizing how much is gone.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little at first, but mostly it feels automatic, not like sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleased or satisfied with yourself after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “pleased” is the word I’d use. I mean… not really? It’s hard to say because afterward I’m usually just sort of aware that it happened, and then my mind jumps to something else.\n\nLike at work, if I finish cleaning the kennels there’s this clear feeling of, okay, that’s done, everything’s in order. With this, it’s not like that. It’s not satisfying in that clean, finished way. But I’m also not sure I can describe exactly what I feel afterward.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I feel pleased with myself. I just don’t have a neat answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, at least at work. There was this bilingual outreach presentation we had to do—well, I was helping with it, and I usually can manage that kind of thing okay, even if I get nervous. But I was so distracted thinking about leaving afterward that I kind of stumbled through parts of it.\n\nAnd then of course I started worrying that my coworkers noticed, which made it worse. I don’t know if they actually did. They might’ve just been focused on the handouts or the animals we were talking about—there was this whole section about proper habitat setup, and people always ask a lot of questions there—but in my head I was like, they can tell I’m not fully here.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it’s affected work some. Relationships, I’m less sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your sexual desire?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking that, honestly.\n\nI guess with animals it’s easy to notice changes because there’s a routine—like if one of the birds stops eating or isn’t acting normal, you can compare it to yesterday. With me, things are less… measurable, I guess.\n\nSo, decreased sexual desire? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything obvious, but I also don’t think I’ve paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disgusted or repelled by sexual activity?\n\nPatient: Repelled? I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say that. I guess I haven’t really thought about it in those words.\n\nLike, “disgusted” makes me think of cleaning cages when something’s gone really wrong, you know, like there’s a smell and your whole body reacts before you even think about it. I don’t think sexual activity feels like that to me. But I also don’t know if I’ve been paying enough attention to answer clearly.\n\nSo… no, not disgusted exactly. I’m just not totally sure what I do feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you enjoy the taste of the food or get pleasure from eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s pleasure, exactly. I mean, sometimes the food tastes good at first, I guess, but during those episodes I’m not sitting there like… savoring it, the way you would with a nice meal.\n\nIt’s more like I notice the food in a quick way, and then my mind kind of goes somewhere else. Like at work when I’m feeding the reptiles and I’m thinking through the whole checklist instead of what I’m actually doing—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it in a normal way. The taste is there, but I’m not sure I’m getting real pleasure from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself having less interest in sexual activity lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “Oh, I’m definitely less interested,” but I also don’t keep track of that very well.\n\nLately my head’s been kind of crowded, and when I’m at work there’s always something to focus on—feeding schedules, cleaning, making sure the animals are acting normal. It’s easier to notice if a snake refuses food than to notice what’s going on with me, I guess.\n\nSo… less interest in sexual activity? I’m not sure. Maybe, maybe not. I haven’t noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I… I don’t know if that’s the word I’d use every time.\n\nSometimes afterward I’m just kind of sitting there thinking, “What was that?” Not in a big dramatic way necessarily. It’s more like when I realize I forgot to latch something properly at work and then I keep replaying it in my head, even if nothing bad happened. Except with food it’s harder to know what I’m replaying, if that makes sense.\n\nSo maybe there’s some guilt mixed in, but I’m not totally sure. I notice feeling off afterward, but I don’t always label it as guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your eating habits are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always notice it as “distress” right away, but it does get into other parts of my day.\n\nLike at work recently, we were doing this bilingual outreach presentation—well, not “we” exactly, I had a part in it—and I kept thinking about leaving afterward, like just getting out of there, and my mind wouldn’t stay on what I was saying. I stumbled through a few parts, which is embarrassing because usually I can talk about the animals pretty comfortably. Then afterward I kept wondering if my coworkers noticed.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it is causing distress. It’s not just staying contained to eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main thing that made me come in.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m by myself at night, and once I start eating it feels like… I don’t know, like something else has the steering wheel. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, put it away, stop now,” and then I’m still standing there eating anyway.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I’m pretty good at stopping and starting tasks—like if one enclosure needs cleaning and then something else comes up, I can switch gears. But with this, once it’s started, it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t sit there timing myself or anything.\n\nSometimes it feels like I sort of look up and realize the food is gone, but I don’t know if that means I was eating really fast or just not paying attention. It’s kind of like when I’m feeding the animals and I’ve done the same steps so many times that suddenly I’m at the end of the row and I don’t remember every single bucket—except this is… different, obviously.\n\nSo, yeah, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed the speed as much as the fact that it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really sure. I know you asked that, and I’m trying to think of it the right way, but I don’t usually notice the pace in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like afterward I realize what happened, and then I’m thinking about other stuff—like whether I left dishes out or whether I’m going to be tired the next morning. I get kind of caught up in those details, the cleanup, the… I don’t know, the routine around it.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say, “yes, I eat really fast.” I just haven’t paid attention to that part clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean, afterward I don’t feel great about it, but I get kind of stuck on the practical stuff first, like cleaning up or making sure there isn’t food out where bugs could get into it.\n\nThat probably sounds silly, but at the animal facility I’m always thinking about sanitation and routines, so my brain goes there automatically. Like, “Did I wipe that down, did I put that away,” that kind of thing.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s guilt in there, but I haven’t really separated it out in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… that part I think I can answer more clearly. It does feel like I don’t have control, or not much.

I haven’t noticed a specific lack of interest, I guess, or difficulty like that, but it’s also not something I’m checking in on very much.\n\nMost days I’m just kind of on autopilot—work, feeding schedules, cleaning enclosures, making sure I didn’t forget some tiny thing. Like last week I kept thinking about one of the older cats who wasn’t acting right, and that stayed in my head all night. So when you ask about sex, I’m sort of like… I don’t know, it hasn’t been at the front of my mind.\n\nSo, no clear problem that I can point to, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the food or the act of eating during these episodes when you feel like you're losing control?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it enjoyment. At the start maybe there’s this… like, pull toward it, like it feels hard to stop thinking about the food. But during it, I don’t know if I’m enjoying it exactly or just doing it.\n\nSometimes I barely even notice what it tastes like, which sounds weird because with animals I can be so tuned in—like if I’m prepping diets and one of the reptiles needs something specific, I’m careful about every detail. But with me it’s kind of the opposite. I’m just eating and then suddenly realizing how much is gone.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little at first, but mostly it feels automatic, not like sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleased or satisfied with yourself after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “pleased” is the word I’d use. I mean… not really? It’s hard to say because afterward I’m usually just sort of aware that it happened, and then my mind jumps to something else.\n\nLike at work, if I finish cleaning the kennels there’s this clear feeling of, okay, that’s done, everything’s in order. With this, it’s not like that. It’s not satisfying in that clean, finished way. But I’m also not sure I can describe exactly what I feel afterward.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I feel pleased with myself. I just don’t have a neat answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, at least at work. There was this bilingual outreach presentation we had to do—well, I was helping with it, and I usually can manage that kind of thing okay, even if I get nervous. But I was so distracted thinking about leaving afterward that I kind of stumbled through parts of it.\n\nAnd then of course I started worrying that my coworkers noticed, which made it worse. I don’t know if they actually did. They might’ve just been focused on the handouts or the animals we were talking about—there was this whole section about proper habitat setup, and people always ask a lot of questions there—but in my head I was like, they can tell I’m not fully here.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it’s affected work some. Relationships, I’m less sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your sexual desire?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking that, honestly.\n\nI guess with animals it’s easy to notice changes because there’s a routine—like if one of the birds stops eating or isn’t acting normal, you can compare it to yesterday. With me, things are less… measurable, I guess.\n\nSo, decreased sexual desire? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything obvious, but I also don’t think I’ve paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disgusted or repelled by sexual activity?\n\nPatient: Repelled? I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say that. I guess I haven’t really thought about it in those words.\n\nLike, “disgusted” makes me think of cleaning cages when something’s gone really wrong, you know, like there’s a smell and your whole body reacts before you even think about it. I don’t think sexual activity feels like that to me. But I also don’t know if I’ve been paying enough attention to answer clearly.\n\nSo… no, not disgusted exactly. I’m just not totally sure what I do feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you enjoy the taste of the food or get pleasure from eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s pleasure, exactly. I mean, sometimes the food tastes good at first, I guess, but during those episodes I’m not sitting there like… savoring it, the way you would with a nice meal.\n\nIt’s more like I notice the food in a quick way, and then my mind kind of goes somewhere else. Like at work when I’m feeding the reptiles and I’m thinking through the whole checklist instead of what I’m actually doing—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it in a normal way. The taste is there, but I’m not sure I’m getting real pleasure from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself having less interest in sexual activity lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “Oh, I’m definitely less interested,” but I also don’t keep track of that very well.\n\nLately my head’s been kind of crowded, and when I’m at work there’s always something to focus on—feeding schedules, cleaning, making sure the animals are acting normal. It’s easier to notice if a snake refuses food than to notice what’s going on with me, I guess.\n\nSo… less interest in sexual activity? I’m not sure. Maybe, maybe not. I haven’t noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I… I don’t know if that’s the word I’d use every time.\n\nSometimes afterward I’m just kind of sitting there thinking, “What was that?” Not in a big dramatic way necessarily. It’s more like when I realize I forgot to latch something properly at work and then I keep replaying it in my head, even if nothing bad happened. Except with food it’s harder to know what I’m replaying, if that makes sense.\n\nSo maybe there’s some guilt mixed in, but I’m not totally sure. I notice feeling off afterward, but I don’t always label it as guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your eating habits are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always notice it as “distress” right away, but it does get into other parts of my day.\n\nLike at work recently, we were doing this bilingual outreach presentation—well, not “we” exactly, I had a part in it—and I kept thinking about leaving afterward, like just getting out of there, and my mind wouldn’t stay on what I was saying. I stumbled through a few parts, which is embarrassing because usually I can talk about the animals pretty comfortably. Then afterward I kept wondering if my coworkers noticed.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it is causing distress. It’s not just staying contained to eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main thing that made me come in.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m by myself at night, and once I start eating it feels like… I don’t know, like something else has the steering wheel. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, put it away, stop now,” and then I’m still standing there eating anyway.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I’m pretty good at stopping and starting tasks—like if one enclosure needs cleaning and then something else comes up, I can switch gears. But with this, once it’s started, it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t sit there timing myself or anything.\n\nSometimes it feels like I sort of look up and realize the food is gone, but I don’t know if that means I was eating really fast or just not paying attention. It’s kind of like when I’m feeding the animals and I’ve done the same steps so many times that suddenly I’m at the end of the row and I don’t remember every single bucket—except this is… different, obviously.\n\nSo, yeah, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed the speed as much as the fact that it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really sure. I know you asked that, and I’m trying to think of it the right way, but I don’t usually notice the pace in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like afterward I realize what happened, and then I’m thinking about other stuff—like whether I left dishes out or whether I’m going to be tired the next morning. I get kind of caught up in those details, the cleanup, the… I don’t know, the routine around it.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say, “yes, I eat really fast.” I just haven’t paid attention to that part clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean, afterward I don’t feel great about it, but I get kind of stuck on the practical stuff first, like cleaning up or making sure there isn’t food out where bugs could get into it.\n\nThat probably sounds silly, but at the animal facility I’m always thinking about sanitation and routines, so my brain goes there automatically. Like, “Did I wipe that down, did I put that away,” that kind of thing.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s guilt in there, but I haven’t really separated it out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I think I can answer more clearly. It does feel like I don’t have control, or not much.\n\nLike, once I’m alone at night and I start eating, it’s like something kind of takes over. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, put this away, you’ve had enough,” and then I’m still opening something else or going back for more.\n\nIt’s weird because during the day I can follow routines really well—at work, if there’s a feeding schedule or cleaning steps, I just do them. But at night with food, it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m telling myself to stop and my body just… doesn’t listen."
